Tocilizumab: (Moderate) Utilize caution with concomitant use of tocilizumab and CYP3A4 substrate drugs, such as combined hormonal oral contraceptives, where a decrease in effectiveness is undesirable. Inhibition of IL-6 signaling by tocilizumab may restore CYP450 activities to higher levels leading to increased metabolism of drugs that are CYP450 substrates as compared to metabolism prior to treatment. This effect on CYP450 enzyme activity may persist for several weeks after stopping tocilizumab. In vitro, tocilizumab has the potential to affect expression of multiple CYP enzymes, including CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4.
Tolterodine: (Moderate) Consider whether the benefits of androgen deprivation therapy outweigh the potential risks in patients receiving other QT prolonging agents. Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may prolong the QT/QTc interval. Tolterodine has been associated with dose-dependent prolongation of the QT interval, especially in poor CYP2D6 metabolizers.
Topiramate: (Major) Advise patients taking estrogen hormones for contraception to consider an alternate or additional form of contraception, such as nonhormonal and/or barrier methods, during and for at least 1 month following discontinuation of topiramate especially when topiramate is used at a dosage of greater than 200 mg per day. Higher-dose hormonal regimens containing a minimum of 30 mcg of ethinyl estradiol or equivalent may also be considered. Patients taking these hormones for other indications may need to be monitored for reduced clinical effect while on topiramate, with dose adjustments made based on clinical efficacy. Topiramate may decrease estrogen hormone concentrations which may decrease efficacy and increase the risk for breakthrough bleeding in patients taking estrogen hormones for contraception. Estrogens are CYP3A substrates and topiramate is a CYP3A inducer. Concomitant use has been observed to decrease ethinyl estradiol exposure by 18%, 21%, and 30% at topiramate daily doses of 200 mg, 400 mg, and 800 mg, respectively. No significant changes in ethinyl estradiol exposure have been observed at topiramate doses of 50 mg to 200 mg per day. (Moderate) Patients taking progestin hormones for contraception may consider an alternate or additional form of contraception, such as nonhormonal and/or barrier methods, during and for 1 month following discontinuation of topiramate. Higher-dose hormonal regimens may also be considered. Monitor patients taking these hormones for other indications for reduced clinical effect while on topiramate; adjust drug dosage as appropriate based on clinical response. Progestins are CYP3A substrates and topiramate is a CYP3A inducer. Pharmacokinetic drug interaction studies have generally shown minimal impact on progestin concentrations especially at topiramate doses of 200 mg/day or less.
Toremifene: (Major) Avoid coadministration of relugolix with toremifene due to the risk of additive QT prolongation. If concomitant use is unavoidable, closely monitor ECGs for QT prolongation and monitor electrolytes. Toremifene has been shown to prolong the QTc interval in a dose- and concentration-related manner. Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may also prolong the QT/QTc interval. (Major) The use of estrogens, including oral contraceptives, with toremifene is controversial and is generally considered contraindicated in most, but not all, circumstances. The use of estrogens may aggravate conditions for which toremifene is prescribed. Toremifene exerts its effects by blocking estrogen receptors. Since toremifene and estrogens are pharmacological opposites, they are not usually given concurrently.
Tovorafenib: (Major) Advise patients taking estrogen hormones for contraception to consider an alternate or additional form of contraception, such as nonhormonal and/or barrier methods, during and for at least 1 month following discontinuation of tovorafenib. Higher-dose hormonal regimens containing a minimum of 30 mcg of ethinyl estradiol or equivalent may also be considered. Patients taking these hormones for other indications may need to be monitored for reduced clinical effect while on tovorafenib, with dose adjustments made based on clinical efficacy. Estrogens are CYP3A substrates and tovorafenib is a CYP3A inducer. Concurrent administration may increase estrogen elimination. (Major) Advise patients taking progestin hormones for contraception to consider an alternate or additional form of contraception, such as nonhormonal and/or barrier methods, during and for at least 1 month following discontinuation of tovorafenib. Higher-dose hormonal regimens may also be considered. Patients taking these hormones for other indications may need to be monitored for reduced clinical effect while on tovorafenib, with dose adjustments made based on clinical efficacy. Progestins are CYP3A substrates and tovorafenib is a CYP3A inducer. Concurrent administration may increase progestin elimination.
Trandolapril; Verapamil: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of relugolix and oral verapamil. Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is unavoidable, administer verapamil at least 6 hours after relugolix and monitor for adverse reactions. Relugolix is a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate and verapamil is a P-gp inhibitor. (Minor) Verapamil inhibits CYP3A4 activity. Serum estrogen concentrations and estrogenic-related side effects (e.g., nausea, breast tenderness) may potentially increase when verapamil is coadministered with either estrogens or combined hormonal contraceptives.
Tranexamic Acid: (Contraindicated) Tranexamic acid is contraindicated in women who are using combination hormonal contraception containing an estrogen and a progestin. Use with other estrogens is also not recommended. Estrogens increase the hepatic synthesis of prothrombin and factors VII, VIII, IX, and X and decrease antithrombin III; estrogens also increase norepinephrine-induced platelet aggregability. A positive relationship of estrogens to thromboembolic disease has been demonstrated, and the US FDA has suggested class labeling of combined OCs and non-oral combination contraceptives in accordance with this data. OC products containing >= 50-mcg ethinyl estradiol are associated with the greatest risk of thromboembolic complications. Therefore, do not coadminister estrogens, combined hormonal oral contraceptives, or non-oral combination contraceptives together with tranexamic acid. Tranexamic acid is an antifibrinolytic agent, and concomitant use can further exacerbate the thrombotic risk associated with these estrogen-containing hormonal products; in post-market use of tranexamic acid, cases of thromboembolic events have been reported, with cases occurring in those patients concomitantly receiving combined hormonal contraceptives containing both an estrogen and a progestin.
Trastuzumab; Hyaluronidase: (Minor) Estrogens, when given in large systemic doses, may render tissues partially resistant to the action of hyaluronidase. Patients receiving these medications may require larger amounts of hyaluronidase for equivalent dispersing effect.
traZODone: (Major) Concomitant use of trazodone and relugolix increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Triamcinolone: (Moderate) Monitor for corticosteroid-related adverse events if corticosteroids are used with estrogens. Concurrent use may increase the exposure of corticosteroids. Estrogens may decrease the hepatic clearance of corticosteroids thereby increasing their effect.
Triclabendazole: (Moderate) Concomitant use of triclabendazole and androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may increase the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) in some patients. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk of QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as avoidance, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, and ECG monitoring, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP.
Trifluoperazine: (Minor) Trifluoperazine is associated with a possible risk for QT prolongation. Theoretically, trifluoperazine may increase the risk of QT prolongation if coadministered with other drugs that have a risk of QT prolongation. Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may also prolong the QT/QTc interval.
Trimethoprim: (Moderate) It would be prudent to recommend alternative or additional contraception when oral contraceptives (OCs) are used in conjunction with antibiotics. Tucatinib: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of relugolix and oral tucatinib. Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is unavoidable, administer tucatinib at least 6 hours after relugolix and monitor for adverse reactions. Relugolix is a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate and tucatinib is a P-gp inhibitor.
Ulipristal: (Major) Avoid concurrent use of ulipristal and progestin-containing hormonal contraceptives or other progestins. Hormonal contraceptives may be started or resumed no sooner than 5 days after ulipristal treatment. Also, a reliable barrier method of contraception should be used during the same menstrual cycle in which ulipristal was administered (until the next menstrual period). Progestin-containing contraceptives may impair the ability of ulipristal to delay ovulation. Ulipristal may reduce the effectiveness of progestin-containing hormonal contraceptives by competitively binding at the progesterone receptor.
Ursodeoxycholic Acid, Ursodiol: (Minor) Estrogens and combined hormonal and oral contraceptives increase hepatic cholesterol secretion, and encourage cholesterol gallstone formation, and hence may counteract the effectiveness of ursodeoxycholic acid, ursodiol.
Vancomycin: (Moderate) It would be prudent to recommend alternative or additional contraception when oral contraceptives (OCs) are used in conjunction with antibiotics. Vandetanib: (Major) Avoid coadministration of vandetanib with relugolix due to an increased risk of QT prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). If concomitant use is unavoidable, monitor ECGs for QT prolongation and monitor electrolytes; correct hypocalcemia, hypomagnesemia, and/or hypokalemia prior to vandetanib administration. An interruption of vandetanib therapy or dose reduction may be necessary for QT prolongation. Vandetanib can prolong the QT interval in a concentration-dependent manner; TdP and sudden death have been reported in patients receiving vandetanib. Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may also prolong the QT/QTc interval.
Vanzacaftor; Tezacaftor; Deutivacaftor: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of relugolix and oral vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor. Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is necessary, administer vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor at least 6 hours after relugolix and monitor for adverse reactions. Alternatively, relugolix therapy may be interrupted for up to 14 days if a short course of vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor is required; if treatment is interrupted for more than 7 days, resume relugolix with a 360 mg loading dose followed by 120 mg once daily. Relugolix is a P-gp substrate and vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor is a P-gp inhibitor.

Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. Relugolix is a P-gp substrate and vanzacaftor; tezacaftor; deutivacaftor is a P-gp inhibitor.
Vardenafil: (Moderate) Concomitant use of vardenafil and androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may increase the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) in some patients. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk of QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as avoidance, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, and ECG monitoring, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP.
Vemurafenib: (Major) Avoid vemurafenib in patients receiving medications known to prolong the QT interval such as relugolix. Concomitant use may also increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. If concomitant oral use is necessary, administer vemurafenib at least six hours after relugolix and monitor for adverse reactions. Vemurafenib is a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) inhibitor that has been shown to prolong the QT interval in a concentration-dependent manner. The ECG changes occurred within the first month of treatment. Relugolix is a P-gp substrate that may also prolong the QT/QTc interval.
Venetoclax: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of relugolix and oral venetoclax. Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is unavoidable, administer venetoclax at least 6 hours after relugolix and monitor for adverse reactions. Relugolix is a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate and venetoclax is a P-gp inhibitor.
Venlafaxine: (Moderate) Concomitant use of venlafaxine and androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may increase the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) in some patients. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk of QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as avoidance, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, and ECG monitoring, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP.
Vepdegestrant: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of vepdegestrant and relugolix. If concomitant use is necessary, administer vepdegestrant at least 6 hours after relugolix and consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP), such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring. Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk for relugolix-related adverse effects and increases the risk for QT/QTc prolongation and TdP. Relugolix is a P-gp substrate, vepdegestrant is a P-gp inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation.
Verapamil: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of relugolix and oral verapamil. Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is unavoidable, administer verapamil at least 6 hours after relugolix and monitor for adverse reactions. Relugolix is a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate and verapamil is a P-gp inhibitor. (Minor) Verapamil inhibits CYP3A4 activity. Serum estrogen concentrations and estrogenic-related side effects (e.g., nausea, breast tenderness) may potentially increase when verapamil is coadministered with either estrogens or combined hormonal contraceptives.
Vimseltinib: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of relugolix and oral vimseltinib. Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is necessary, administer vimseltinib at least 6 hours after relugolix and monitor for adverse reactions. Alternatively, relugolix therapy may be interrupted for up to 14 days if a short course of vimseltinib is required; if treatment is interrupted for more than 7 days, resume relugolix with a 360 mg loading dose followed by 120 mg once daily. Relugolix is a P-gp substrate and vimseltinib is a P-gp inhibitor.
Voclosporin: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of relugolix and oral voclosporin. Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. Additive QT prolongation may also occur. If concomitant use is necessary, administer voclosporin at least six hours after relugolix and monitor for adverse reactions. Alternatively, relugolix therapy may be interrupted for up to 14 days if a short course of voclosporin is required; if treatment is interrupted for more than seven days, resume relugolix with a 360 mg loading dose followed by 120 mg once daily. Relugolix is a P-gp substrate that may prolong the QT/QTc interval; voclosporin is a P-gp inhibitor that has been associated with QT prolongation at supratherapeutic doses.
Vonoprazan; Amoxicillin: (Moderate) It would be prudent to recommend alternative or additional contraception when oral contraceptives (OCs) are used in conjunction with antibiotics. It was previously thought that antibiotics may decrease the effectiveness of OCs containing estrogens due to stimulation of metabolism or a reduction in enterohepatic circulation via changes in GI flora. One retrospective study reviewed the literature to determine the effects of oral antibiotics on the pharmacokinetics of contraceptive estrogens and progestins, and also examined clinical studies in which the incidence of pregnancy with OCs and antibiotics was reported. It was concluded that the antibiotics ampicillin, ciprofloxacin, clarithromycin, doxycycline, metronidazole, ofloxacin, roxithromycin, temafloxacin, and tetracycline did not alter plasma concentrations of OCs. Antituberculous drugs (e.g., rifampin) were the only agents associated with OC failure and pregnancy. Based on the study results, these authors recommended that back-up contraception may not be necessary if OCs are used reliably during oral antibiotic use. Another review concurred with these data, but noted that individual patients have been identified who experienced significant decreases in plasma concentrations of combined OC components and who appeared to ovulate; the agents most often associated with these changes were rifampin, tetracyclines, and penicillin derivatives. These authors concluded that because females most at risk for OC failure or noncompliance may not be easily identified and the true incidence of such events may be under-reported, and given the serious consequence of unwanted pregnancy, that recommending an additional method of contraception during short-term antibiotic use may be justified. During long-term antibiotic administration, the risk for drug interaction with OCs is less clear, but alternative or additional contraception may be advisable in selected circumstances. Data regarding progestin-only contraceptives or for newer combined contraceptive deliveries (e.g., patches, rings) are not available.
Vonoprazan; Amoxicillin; Clarithromycin: (Major) Avoid coadministration of clarithromycin with relugolix. Clarithromycin is associated with an established risk for QT prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may also prolong the QT/QTc interval. Also, concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. Relugolix therapy may be interrupted for up to 14 days if a short course of clarithromycin is required; if treatment is interrupted for more than 7 days, resume relugolix with a 360 mg loading dose followed by 120 mg once daily. Relugolix is a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate and clarithromycin is a P-gp inhibitor. (Moderate) It would be prudent to recommend alternative or additional contraception when oral contraceptives (OCs) are used in conjunction with antibiotics. It was previously thought that antibiotics may decrease the effectiveness of OCs containing estrogens due to stimulation of metabolism or a reduction in enterohepatic circulation via changes in GI flora. (Moderate) It would be prudent to recommend alternative or additional contraception when oral contraceptives (OCs) are used in conjunction with antibiotics. It was previously thought that antibiotics may decrease the effectiveness of OCs containing estrogens due to stimulation of metabolism or a reduction in enterohepatic circulation via changes in GI flora. One retrospective study reviewed the literature to determine the effects of oral antibiotics on the pharmacokinetics of contraceptive estrogens and progestins, and also examined clinical studies in which the incidence of pregnancy with OCs and antibiotics was reported. Another review concurred with these data, but noted that individual patients have been identified who experienced significant decreases in plasma concentrations of combined OC components and who appeared to ovulate; the agents most often associated with these changes were rifampin, tetracyclines, and penicillin derivatives. These authors concluded that because females most at risk for OC failure or noncompliance may not be easily identified and the true incidence of such events may be under-reported, and given the serious consequence of unwanted pregnancy, that recommending an additional method of contraception during short-term antibiotic use may be justified. During long-term antibiotic administration, the risk for drug interaction with OCs is less clear, but alternative or additional contraception may be advisable in selected circumstances. Data regarding progestin-only contraceptives or for newer combined contraceptive deliveries (e.g., patches, rings) are not available. In addition, drospirenone has antimineralocorticoid effects; the progestin may increase serum potassium. Consider monitoring serum potassium concentrations during the first month of dosing in high-risk patients who take strong CYP3A4 inhibitors long-term and concomitantly. Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors include clarithromycin. (Minor) Estrogens are partially metabolized by CYP3A4. Drugs that inhibit CYP3A4 such as clarithromycin may increase plasma concentrations of estrogens and cause estrogen-related side effects such as nausea and breast tenderness. Patients receiving estrogens should be monitored for an increase in adverse events. Also, practitioners should be alert to the possibility that breakthrough bleeding or contraceptive failure may occur with clarithromycin.
Vorasidenib: (Major) Advise patients taking estrogen hormones for contraception to consider an alternate or additional form of contraception, such as nonhormonal and/or barrier methods, during and for at least 1 month following discontinuation of vorasidenib. Higher-dose hormonal regimens containing a minimum of 30 mcg of ethinyl estradiol or equivalent may also be considered. Patients taking these hormones for other indications may need to be monitored for reduced clinical effect while on vorasidenib, with dose adjustments made based on clinical response. Estrogens are CYP3A substrates and vorasidenib is a CYP3A inducer. Concurrent administration may increase estrogen elimination. (Major) Advise patients taking progestin hormones for contraception to consider an alternate or additional form of contraception, such as nonhormonal and/or barrier methods, during and for at least 1 month following discontinuation of vorasidenib. Higher-dose hormonal regimens may also be considered. Patients taking these hormones for other indications may need to be monitored for reduced clinical effect while on vorasidenib, with dose adjustments made based on clinical efficacy. Progestins are CYP3A substrates and vorasidenib is a CYP3A inducer. Concurrent administration may increase progestin elimination.
Voriconazole: (Moderate) Administer voriconazole with caution in combination with relugolix due to the risk of additive QT prolongation. Voriconazole has been associated with QT prolongation and rare cases of torsade de pointes. Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may also prolong the QT/QTc interval. (Moderate) Ethinyl estradiol and norethindrone increases the Cmax and AUC of voriconazole and voriconazole increases the Cmax and AUC of both ethinyl estradiol and norethindrone. (Minor) As voriconazole inhibits CYP3A4 activity, serum estrogen concentrations and estrogenic-related side effects (e.g., nausea, breast tenderness) may potentially increase when coadministered with either estrogens or combined hormonal contraceptives.
Vorinostat: (Moderate) Consider whether the benefits of androgen deprivation therapy outweigh the potential risks in patients receiving other QT prolonging agents. Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may prolong the QT/QTc interval. Vorinostat therapy is associated with a risk of QT prolongation.
Warfarin: (Moderate) Closely monitor the INR if coadministration of warfarin with estrogens is necessary as concurrent use may increase the exposure of warfarin leading to increased bleeding risk. The R-enantiomer of warfarin is a CYP1A2 substrate and estrogens are CYP1A2 inhibitors. The S-enantiomer of warfarin exhibits 2 to 5 times more anticoagulant activity than the R-enantiomer, but the R-enantiomer generally has a slower clearance.
Xanomeline; Trospium: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of relugolix and oral xanomeline. Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is necessary, administer xanomeline at least 6 hours after relugolix and monitor for adverse reactions. Alternatively, relugolix therapy may be interrupted for up to 14 days if a short course of xanomeline is required; if treatment is interrupted for more than 7 days, resume relugolix with a 360 mg loading dose followed by 120 mg once daily. Relugolix is a P-gp substrate and xanomeline is a P-gp inhibitor.
Zafirlukast: (Minor) As zarfirlukast inhibits CYP3A4 activity, serum estrogen concentrations and estrogenic-related side effects (e.g., nausea, breast tenderness) may potentially increase when coadministered with either estrogens or combined hormonal contraceptives.
Zidesamtinib: (Moderate) Concomitant use of zidesamtinib and androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may increase the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) in some patients. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk of QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as avoidance, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, and ECG monitoring, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP.
Ziftomenib: (Major) Concomitant use of ziftomenib and androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Ziprasidone: (Major) Concomitant use of ziprasidone and relugolix should be avoided due to a potential for additive QT prolongation. Clinical trial data indicate that ziprasidone causes QT prolongation; there are postmarketing reports of torsade de pointes (TdP) in patients with multiple confounding factors. Androgen deprivation therapy (i.e., relugolix) may also prolong the QT/QTc interval.
Zonisamide: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of relugolix and zonisamide. Concomitant use may increase relugolix exposure and the risk of relugolix-related adverse effects. If concomitant use is unavoidable, administer zonisamide at least six hours after relugolix and monitor for adverse reactions. Relugolix is a P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate and zonisamide is a P-gp inhibitor.

Adverse Reactions
  Headline
  Adverse Reactions
  Adverse Reactions
    Headline
    osteopenia, osteoporosis
    General Information
    Relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate may cause a decrease in BMD in premenopausal patients. In clinical trials for uterine fibroids or endometriosis, women treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate for up to 52 weeks had a decline in lumbar spine BMD of 0.8%. BMD loss may be greater with increasing duration of use and may not be completely reversible after stopping treatment. In a clinical trial, 2 women treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate sustained fractures after falling. One woman sustained a tibia/fibula fracture following 24 weeks of relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate treatment followed by 12 weeks of relugolix monotherapy. The other woman sustained a wrist fracture 144 days after relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate discontinuation. The impact of BMD decreases on long-term bone health and future bone fracture risk in premenopausal women is unknown. Consider the assessment of BMD in patients with a history of low-trauma fracture or other risk factors for osteoporosis, osteopenia, or bone loss, including those patients taking other medications that may decrease BMD. An assessment of BMD by dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry (DXA) is recommended at baseline. In women with heavy menstrual bleeding associated with uterine fibroids, periodic DXA during treatment is recommended. In women with endometriosis, annual DXA is recommended. Consider discontinuing therapy if the risk associated with bone loss exceeds the potential benefit of treatment. Limit the duration of use to 24 months to reduce the extent of bone loss. Although the effect of supplementation with calcium and vitamin D was not studied, such supplementation may be beneficial for all patients.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms

    Headline
    amenorrhea, hot flashes, hyperhidrosis, libido decrease, menorrhagia, menstrual irregularity, night sweats, pelvic pain, vaginal bleeding
    General Information
    In general, relugolix reduces menstrual bleeding in premenopausal individuals as a result of its pharmacologic effect. In clinical studies for uterine fibroids, a greater proportion (50% to 50.4%) of relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate recipients experienced amenorrhea compared to placebo. Abnormal uterine bleeding (including menorrhagia, metrorrhagia, vaginal bleeding/hemorrhage, polymenorrhea, and menstrual irregularity) was reported in 6.3% of subjects treated for uterine fibroids and 6.7% of subjects treated for endometriosis and at a greater incidence than with placebo. Uterine hemorrhage was among the serious adverse reactions reported in 2.9% of treated subjects receiving treatment for endometriosis. The most common adverse reaction leading to discontinuation of relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate for uterine fibroids was uterine bleeding (1.2%), with onset usually reported within the first 3 months of therapy. Libido decrease (includes loss of libido) was reported in 3.1% of those receiving the regimen for uterine fibroids and 4.3% of subjects treated for endometriosis. Hot flashes, hyperhidrosis, or night sweats were reported in 10.6% treated for uterine fibroids and 13.2% of subjects treated for endometriosis. Vulvovaginal dryness (vaginal atrophy) was reported in 2.2% of subjects receiving relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone for endometriosis. Serious adverse drug reactions included uterine myoma expulsion and menorrhagia experienced by 1 subject, and uterine leiomyoma (prolapse) and pelvic pain reported for 1 subject each.

    Headline
    abdominal pain, diarrhea, dyspepsia, nausea, vomiting
    General Information
    Dyspepsia was reported in 2% to 3% of women receiving relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate for uterine fibroids. Nausea was reported in 6% and diarrhea was reported in 2.4% of women treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate for endometriosis, and at incidences higher than with placebo. Estrogen and progestin combination products may cause nausea, vomiting, and abdominal pain which may indicate a need for decreased dosage or discontinuation of these hormones if these symptoms persist.

    Headline
    myocardial infarction, pulmonary embolism, stroke, thromboembolism, thrombosis
    General Information
    In clinical trials with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate for endometriosis, 2 thromboembolic events (DVT and PE) occurred in one woman with risk factors of obesity and a preceding knee injury and one case was reported for a woman treated with relugolix monotherapy postmarketing. Discontinue relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate treatment immediately if an arterial or venous thrombotic, cardiovascular, or cerebrovascular event occurs or is suspected. Deep and superficial venous thrombosis, pulmonary embolism (PE), thrombophlebitis, myocardial infarction, and stroke have been reported with estrogen and/or progestin therapy. The use of estrogens, with or without a progestin, carries a risk for thromboembolism, and cardiovascular events such as myocardial infarction (MI) or stroke, particularly in women with risk factors for these events. In general, the risk is greatest among women over 35 years of age who smoke, and women with uncontrolled high blood pressure, dyslipidemia, vascular disease, or obesity.

    Headline
    fluid retention, hypertension, peripheral edema, weight gain
    General Information
    In clinical trials for uterine fibroids or endometriosis, more women experienced the adverse reaction of new or worsening hypertension with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate as compared to placebo (7% vs. 0.8%). Fluid retention may cause weight gain/weight fluctuation in patients taking estrogens and/or progestins and may aggravate pre-existing high blood pressure. Peripheral edema was reported in 2.2% of women receiving relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate for endometriosis. For women with well-controlled hypertension, continue to monitor blood pressure and stop the use of this product if blood pressure rises significantly. Monitor blood pressure periodically in normotensive patients treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate.

    Headline
    diplopia, headache, migraine, papilledema, retinal thrombosis, visual impairment
    General Information
    Headache and migraine have been reported with estrogens and/or progestin therapy; changes in pre-existing headache or migraine patterns may occur with hormonal therapy. Headache was reported in 33% of women receiving relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate for endometriosis vs. 26.4% receiving placebo. Discontinue therapy pending examination if there is sudden onset of migraine or a worsening of migraine or headache patterns. A severe headache with focal neurologic symptoms may be a warning sign of a serious adverse event such as a stroke or retinal thrombosis. Stop therapy immediately if there is sudden unexplained partial or complete loss of vision (visual impairment), proptosis, diplopia, papilledema, or retinal vascular lesions and evaluate for retinal thrombosis (retinal vein thrombosis) as these have been reported in patients receiving estrogens and progestins. If examination reveals a serious event, therapy should be permanently discontinued.

    Headline
    alopecia
    General Information
    In a clinical trial for uterine fibroids, relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate, 3.5% of women experienced alopecia, hair loss, and hair thinning compared to 0.8% of women receiving placebo. In 3 of the 11 affected women, alopecia was reported as moderate. One woman in the extension trial discontinued treatment because of alopecia. No specific pattern of hair loss was described. The majority of affected women completed the study with reported hair loss ongoing. Whether the hair loss is reversible is unknown. Consider discontinuing therapy if hair loss becomes a concern.

    Headline
    anxiety, depression, emotional lability, irritability, suicidal ideation
    General Information
    Adverse mood changes were reported in trials with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate. In clinical trials for uterine fibroids, a greater proportion of women treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate compared to placebo reported depression (including depression, mood swings or emotional lability, and depressed mood) (2.4% vs. 0.8%), irritability (2.4% vs. 0%), and anxiety (1.2% vs. 0.8%). Mood-related disorders were the most common reason for discontinuation of relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate in women with endometriosis. Mood disorders (including emotional lability, affective disorder, anxiety, depressed mood, depression, emotional distress, generalized anxiety disorder, irritability, mixed anxiety and depressive disorder, altered mood, mood swings, and suicidal ideation) were reported in 9.1% of women treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate for endometriosis compared to 7.2% of women receiving placebo. Suicidal ideation was among the serious adverse reactions reported in 2.9% of women receiving relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone for endometriosis; the women who reported suicidal ideation had a history of depression and/or anxiety.

    Headline
    hypercholesterolemia, hypertriglyceridemia, pancreatitis
    General Information
    Use of relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate may cause hyperlipidemia; treatment is associated with increases in total cholesterol and low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C). In women with pre-existing hyperlipidemia or hypertriglyceridemia, estrogen therapy may be associated with elevations of plasma triglycerides and may lead to pancreatitis. Lipid levels were assessed at baseline and the end of treatment (Week 24). Of the women treated for uterine fibroids with normal total cholesterol (less than 200 mg/dL) at baseline, increases to greater than 200 to 240 mg/dL were seen in 13.7% of women treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate as compared to 7.7% of women treated with placebo, and increases to greater than 240 mg/dL were seen in 1.7% and 0.6% of relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate and placebo-treated women respectively. For women treated for uterine fibroids with LDL less than 130 mg/dL at baseline, increases to 130 to less than 160 mg/dL, 160 to less than 190 mg/dL and 190 mg/dL and greater were seen in 9.3%, 1.5%, and 0.5% of women treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate vs. 6.5%, 0.5% and 0% of women treated with placebo, respectively. Of the women treated for endometriosis with normal total cholesterol (less than 200 mg/dL) at baseline, increases to greater than 200 to 240 mg/dL were seen in 13.6% of women treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate as compared to 9.3% of women treated with placebo, and increases to greater than 240 mg/dL were seen in 0.7% and 1% of relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate and placebo-treated women respectively. For women treated for endometriosis with LDL less than 130 mg/dL at baseline, increases to 130 to less than 160 mg/dL, 160 to less than 190 mg/dL and 190 mg/dL and greater were seen in 8%, 0.3%, and 0% of women treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate vs. 7.6%, 0% and 0% of women treated with placebo, respectively. Among women treated for endometriosis with normal HDL at baseline (60 mg/dL or greater), declines to 40 to less than 60 mg/dL occurred in 22.2% of women treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate compared to 12.2% (27/221) of women receiving placebo. Monitor lipid levels during treatment and consider discontinuation of treatment if hypercholesterolemia or hypertriglyceridemia worsens or if pancreatitis occurs.

    Headline
    biliary obstruction, cholecystitis, cholelithiasis, cholestasis, elevated hepatic enzymes, jaundice
    General Information
    Elevated hepatic enzymes have been reported in patients receiving relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate. In clinical trials for the treatment of uterine fibroids or endometriosis, elevations [3 times the upper limit of the normal (ULN) or more] in alanine aminotransferase (ALT) occurred in 0.4% (1/254) and 0.7% (3/418) of patients treated with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate, vs. 0% and 0.5% in placebo-treated patients, respectively. Elevations of 3 times the ULN or more in aspartate aminotransferase (AST) occurred in 0.8% (2/254) and 0.2% (1/418) of treated patients vs. 0.4% (1/256) and 0.5% (2/416) for placebo, respectively. No pattern in time to onset of the elevated liver function tests (LFTs) was identified. Advise patients to promptly seek medical attention in case of symptoms/signs that may reflect liver injury, such as jaundice. Studies among estrogen users suggest a small increased relative risk of developing gallbladder disease. Estrogens enhance hepatic lipoprotein uptake and inhibit bile acid synthesis, resulting in increased concentration of cholesterol in the bile which can lead to cholelithiasis, biliary obstruction, cholestasis, and cholestatic jaundice. Cholelithiasis and cholecystitis were among the serious adverse reactions reported in 2.9% of women receiving relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone for endometriosis.

    Headline
    hyperglycemia
    General Information
    Relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate may decrease glucose tolerance due to the estrogen/progestin components of the product and result in hyperglycemia, particularly in at-risk patients. More frequent monitoring in women with prediabetes and diabetes may be needed.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      hyperglycemia
      Severity
      Moderate
    Headline
    hypothyroidism
    General Information
    Use relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate with caution in patients with thyroid disease, particularly hypothyroidism. Estrogens can increase thyroid-binding globulin (TBG) levels, which may reduce free thyroid levels. Patients with normal thyroid function can compensate for the increased TBG by making more thyroid hormone, thus maintaining free T4 and T3 serum concentrations in the normal range. The use of estrogen and progestin combinations may also raise serum concentrations of binding proteins such as corticosteroid-binding globulin, which may reduce corticosteroid hormone levels. Patients with hypothyroidism or adrenal insufficiency may require increased doses of thyroid replacement therapy or cortisol replacement therapy, respectively. These patients should have their condition monitored in order to maintain their hormone levels in an acceptable range.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      hypothyroidism
      Severity
      Moderate
    Headline
    arthralgia, back pain, dental pain, dizziness, fatigue
    General Information
    In clinical trials with patients receiving relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate for endometriosis, toothache (dental pain, 5.5% vs. 2.4% placebo), back pain (4.8% vs. 2.9% placebo), arthralgia (3.6% vs. 2.2% placebo), fatigue (3.1% vs. 2.4% placebo), and dizziness (3.1% vs. 1.2% placebo) were reported.

    Headline
    new primary malignancy
    General Information
    Estrogens and progestins have been associated with the development of new primary malignancy (e.g., breast cancer or other hormonally-responsive malignancies); risks for such malignancies are dependent on many factors, including whether a woman is pre- or postmenopausal. In clinical trials with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate, breast cyst was reported in 2% to 3% of women. Uterine leiomyoma degeneration was reported during postmarketing experience. The use of estrogen-alone and estrogen plus progestin has been reported to result in an increase in abnormal mammograms requiring further evaluation. Surveillance measures, such as breast examinations and regular mammography, are recommended. Discontinue relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate if a hormonally-sensitive malignancy is diagnosed.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      new primary malignancy
      Severity
      Severe

Description
  Text
  Relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate is a combination of relugolix, a gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH) receptor antagonist, with an estrogen (estradiol), and a progestin (norethindrone acetate); the product is indicated for the management of heavy menstrual bleeding associated with uterine leiomyomas (fibroids) and for the management of moderate to severe pain associated with endometriosis in premenopausal women. Both phase 3 studies in women with fibroids met the primary endpoint, with 72.1% and 71.2% of women in the relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate groups achieving the responder criteria compared with 16.8% and 14.7% women in the placebo groups at Week 24, respectively (p less than 0.0001). A response was defined as a menstrual blood loss volume of less than 80 mL and a 50% or greater reduction from baseline in menstrual blood loss volume during the last 35 days of treatment, as measured using the alkaline hematin method. Women receiving relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate experienced reductions of 82% and 84.3% in menstrual blood loss from baselines, respectively (both p is less than 0.0001 compared to placebo). Both phase 3 studies in women with endometriosis each met their co-primary endpoints with 75% of women in the relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate group in both studies achieving a clinically meaningful reduction in dysmenorrhea compared with 27% and 30% of women in the placebo groups at Week 24, respectively (p less than 0.0001). For non-menstrual pelvic pain, treatment with relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate demonstrated a clinically meaningful reduction in pain in 59% and 66% of women, compared with 40% and 43% of women in the placebo groups (p less than 0.0001).

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  The combination of relugolix, estradiol, and norethindrone acetate act together to reduce heavy menstrual bleeding associated with uterine leiomyomas (fibroids) and pain associated with endometriosis in premenopausal women, while off-setting some potential for adverse effects such as bone loss.
Relugolix: Relugolix is a non-peptide GnRH receptor antagonist that competitively binds to pituitary GnRH receptors, thereby reducing the release of luteinizing hormone (LH) and follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH), leading to decreased serum concentrations of the ovarian sex hormones estradiol and progesterone and reduced bleeding associated with uterine fibroids and pain associated with endometriosis.
Estradiol: Estradiol acts by binding to nuclear receptors that are expressed in estrogen-responsive tissues (e.g., female organs, breasts, hypothalamus, pituitary). Estrogens are responsible for the growth and development of female sex organs and the maintenance of sex characteristics including growth of axillary and pubic hair and shaping of body contours and skeleton. Estrogens appear to prevent osteoporosis, primarily by preserving bone mineral density (BMD) in the spine and in the hip. The addition of exogenous estradiol may reduce the increase in bone resorption and resultant bone loss that can occur due to a decrease in circulating estrogen from relugolix alone.
Norethindrone acetate: At the cellular level, norethindrone diffuses freely into target cells and binds to the progesterone receptors in the female reproductive tract, mammary gland, hypothalamus, and pituitary. Progestins, when added to estrogen therapies, attenuate some positive effects of estrogens on HDL-cholesterol, but LDL-cholesterol benefits are retained. Norethindrone is a progestin of low progestational and slight androgenic and estrogenic activity. Progestins such as norethindrone act by binding to nuclear receptors that are expressed in progesterone responsive tissues. The norethindrone component may protect the uterus from the potential adverse endometrial effects of unopposed estrogen, including endometrial carcinoma.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Pharmacokinetic data after oral administration of this product regimen are provided.
Relugolix: The mean Cmax is 26 ng/mL following a single relugolix dose under fasted conditions. The time to peak concentration (Tmax) is 2 hours. The mean AUC is 198.1 ng x hour/mL. The AUC and Cmax are decreased by 38% and 55%, respectively, after ingestion a high-fat meal, high calorie; however, the drug may be given with or without food.
Estradiol: The mean Cmax is 28 ng/mL following a single estradiol dose under fasted conditions. The time to peak concentration (Tmax) is 7 hours. The mean AUC is 818.7 ng x hour/mL. No clinically meaningful effects of food on the exposure to estradiol were observed.
Norethindrone acetate: The mean Cmax is 3.6 ng/mL following a single norethindrone dose. The time to peak concentration (Tmax) is 1 hour. The mean AUC is 17.5 ng x hour/mL. No clinically meaningful effects of food on the exposure to norethindrone were observed.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Data regarding how hepatic impairment affects the pharmacokinetics of the drugs within this combination product are as listed.
Relugolix: No clinically meaningful differences in relugolix pharmacokinetics were observed in patients with mild to moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A or B). The effect of severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh C) has not been evaluated.
Estradiol: The use of estradiol in patients with hepatic impairment, compared to patients with normal hepatic function, is expected to increase the estradiol blood levels.
Norethindrone acetate: The pharmacokinetics of norethindrone acetate in patients with hepatic disease have not been evaluated.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Data regarding how renal impairment affects the pharmacokinetics of the drugs within this combination product are as listed.
Relugolix: No clinically meaningful differences in relugolix pharmacokinetics were observed in patients with mild to severe renal impairment (CrCl 15 to 89 mL/minute). The effect of end-stage renal disease with or without hemodialysis has not been evaluated.
Estradiol: The pharmacokinetics of estradiol in patients with renal impairment have not been evaluated.
Norethindrone acetate: The pharmacokinetics of norethindrone acetate in patients with renal impairment have not been evaluated.
    Name
    Pediatrics
    Description
    Pharmacokinetic data in patients less than 18 years of age are not available.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    No clinically meaningful difference in the pharmacokinetics of relugolix were observed based on Asian, White, and Black/African American subjects. The effect of race/ethnicity on the pharmacokinetics of estradiol or norethindrone acetate has not been studied.
    Name
    Obesity
    Description
    The pharmacokinetics of relugolix is not affected by body weight or body mass index (BMI). The effect of body weight or BMI on the pharmacokinetics of estradiol or norethindrone acetate has not been studied.
  Text
  The relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate product is administered orally.
Relugolix: Relugolix is administered orally. The plasma protein binding of relugolix is 68% to 71%, primarily to albumin and to a lesser extent to alpha1-acid glycoprotein. The mean blood-to-plasma ratio is 0.78. Relugolix is metabolized primarily by CYP3A and to a lesser extent by CYP2C8 in vitro. After oral administration of a single radiolabeled dose, approximately 81% of the radioactivity was recovered in the feces (4.2% as unchanged drug) and 4.1% in the urine (2.2% as unchanged drug). After administration of a single dose of relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate, the mean terminal elimination relugolix half-life is 61.5 hours. The mean total clearance is 29.4 L/hour and the renal clearance is 8 L/hour.
Estradiol: Estradiol is widely distributed and is 98% protein-bound, primarily to albumin and sex hormone-binding globulin (SHBG). Estradiol, estrone, and estriol undergo glucuronide and sulfate conjugation to a variety of minor metabolites that are excreted in the urine. After administration of a single dose of relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate, the mean terminal phase elimination estradiol half-life is approximately 16.6 hours.
Norethindrone acetate: Norethindrone is widely distributed and is 97% protein-bound, primarily to albumin and sex hormone-binding globulin (SHBG). Approximately 10% of norethindrone is metabolized by oxidative reduction, and the remainder via conjugation to the sulfate and glucuronate salts. Excretion of norethindrone as inactive metabolites occurs via the urine. After administration of a single dose of relugolix; estradiol; norethindrone acetate, the mean terminal phase elimination norethindrone acetate half-life is approximately 10.9 hours.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes or transporters: CYP3A4, P-glycoprotein (P-gp)
Relugolix: Relugolix is metabolized primarily by CYP3A and to a lesser extent CYP2C8 in vitro and relugolix is a substrate for intestinal P-gp. Coadministration of relugolix with a P-gp inhibitor increases the AUC and the maximum concentration (Cmax) of relugolix. When relugolix was coadministered with a combined P-gp and moderate CYP3A inhibitor, the relugolix AUC and Cmax increased by 4.1-fold and 3.8-fold, respectively. No clinically meaningful differences in the pharmacokinetics of unconjugated estradiol, total estrone, unconjugated estrone, and norethindrone were observed. Coadministration of relugolix with a combined P-gp and a strong CYP3A inducer decreased the AUC and Cmax of relugolix, reducing the relugolix AUC and Cmax by 55% and 23%, respectively. No clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of either a sensitive CYP3A substrate or a breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) substrate were observed with coadministration of relugolix. In vitro studies show that relugolix is not an inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A4. Additionally, these studies show relugolix is an inducer of CYP3A and CYP2B6, but not CYP1A2. Relugolix is a substrate of P-gp in vitro and is an inhibitor of BCRP and P-gp, but not organic anion transporter protein 1B1 (OATP), OATP1B3, organic anion transporter 1 (OAT), OAT3, organic cation transporter (OCT) 2, multidrug and toxin extrusion 1 (MATE), MATE2, or bile salt export pump (BSEP).
Estradiol: In vitro and in vivo studies indicate that estrogens are partially metabolized by CYP3A4. Interactions with drugs that are strong inducers of CYP3A4 are possible.
Norethindrone acetate: In vitro and in vivo studies indicate that progestins are partially metabolized by CYP3A4. Interactions with drugs that are strong inducers of CYP3A4 are possible.
